Hi, Doctor. Thank you. Well, I came in because... I guess lately I just can't seem to stop focusing on my appearance.

Well, it hasn't stopped me from doing my job or helping out with the trainings. I'm still doing all of that. But I have noticed it takes me longer to get ready on the days I have to stand up in front of everyone. I think... I think I'd try to believe you. If you, a doctor, are telling me that I look normal, I know I should trust that. But... I'd still have that nagging feeling. I'm still doing all of that. But I have noticed it takes me longer to get ready on the days I have to stand up in front of everyone. I don't know for sure, but I do think there's a chance I might be making it worse in my head.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts and worries about your hands and face looking this way? When did this all start?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure when it started, to be honest. It's just been a while now. I can't really put a specific date or even a year on it. It feels like it just sort of crept up on me over time.\n\nDoctor: If I told you that I looked at those photos and your hands and face look completely normal to me, would you be able to accept that, or would you still be convinced that there's something wrong with how you look?\n\nPatient: I think... I think I'd try to believe you. If you, a doctor, are telling me that I look normal, I know I should trust that. But... I'd still have that nagging feeling. Even if I say I accept it, when I go home and look in the mirror again, I know I'll still see it. So, I guess I'd want to believe you, but it would be really hard to just completely let go of the idea that something is wrong.\n\nDoctor: When you're spending that extra time getting ready on training days or avoiding photos, would you say you're spending maybe 30 minutes to an hour on this, or is it taking up significantly more time than that—like several hours a day?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I haven't really looked at the clock to see exactly how long it takes. I'm not sure if it's just a half hour or if it adds up to more than that throughout the day. I just know I spend extra time trying to cover things up before I go out, especially on those training days, but... yeah, I really couldn't give you a specific number of minutes or hours. I just haven't kept track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: When you really think about it honestly, do you believe that other people genuinely cannot see the wrinkles and crookedness you see, or do you think they're just not being truthful with you about what they actually observe?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... I guess I haven't really figured that out. Sometimes I think maybe they genuinely just don't notice it, like they aren't looking at me that closely. But then... well, I don't know, maybe they do see it and they're just being polite by not bringing it up. I really couldn't tell you for sure what they actually see. I just don't know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

My hands don't hurt, and my face feels completely normal. It's really just the way they look that bothers me. There's no physical feeling attached to it at all.\n\nDoctor: When you think about what other people have told you—that they don't see the wrinkles or the crooked face—do you believe them, or do you think they're just being nice and not telling you the truth about how you actually look?\n\nPatient: Well, I... Sometimes I think maybe I am blowing it out of proportion, but when I look in the mirror or see those pictures, it just looks so obvious to me. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: If you were to rate on a scale of 0 to 100, where 0 means 'I'm completely certain my hands and face look exactly as bad as I perceive them' and 100 means 'I'm completely certain I'm exaggerating how bad they look,' where would you place yourself right now?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to put a number on it. I guess I'm just not sure. I mean, I know what I see when I look in the mirror, but after what you said... I don't know if I'm completely certain about anything right now. I just couldn't give you a number on a scale like that.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror right now and see those wrinkles and the crookedness in your face, how convinced are you that it's actually there—are you 100% certain it's real, or is there some doubt?\n\nPatient: I... well, I guess I'm just not completely sure anymore. I mean, when I look in the mirror, I see it. It looks real to me. But... I don't know, maybe I'm not 100% certain. It's hard to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: When you really think about it, do you recognize that your worry about these wrinkles and the crookedness in your face might be excessive or out of proportion to what's actually there, or do you think your concerns are completely justified?\n\nPatient: I mean... Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, putting an exact number of hours on it is a little tough. But it's definitely more than just popping into my head here and there. Actually, that's really why I came in. 

Since I started helping out with the safety trainings at work, I just... I can’t seem to stop noticing it. When I’m up there pointing things out on the floor, all I can focus on is how wrinkly and uneven my hands look. It stays on my mind a lot while I'm trying to work. And then I start thinking about how my face looks all crooked in the company newsletter photos, even though the other workers tell me they don’t see it at all. 

So, I guess if you add it up, it's quite a few hours throughout the day where I'm just distracted by it and worrying about how I look to everyone else.

And then... well, they put pictures of the trainings in the company newsletter, and when I look at them, I start thinking my face looks crooked in the photos. \n\nOther people tell me they don't see it at all, but I just can't seem to let it go. It's on my mind a lot, so I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How much is this concern about your hands and face affecting your daily life? For example, is it interfering with your work, your ability to be around others, or how much time you're spending thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped me from doing my job or helping out with the trainings. I'm still doing all of that. But I have noticed it takes me longer to get ready on the days I have to stand up in front of everyone. I spend a lot of time trying to make sure I look okay, maybe trying to cover up my hands a bit or fix my hair so my face doesn't look so uneven. \n\nAnd I've definitely started trying to avoid the camera when they're taking pictures for the newsletter. I'll just step out of the frame or turn away. I guess I've also been a little more hesitant about going to some of the get-togethers after work. I still go sometimes, but I just don't enjoy it as much as I used to because I'm always thinking about how I look. So it's not ruining my life, but it's definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands and face, do you notice any actual physical sensations like pain, tingling, numbness, or tension in those areas, or is it mainly just the way they look that bothers you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any pain or tingling or anything like that. My hands don't hurt, and my face feels completely normal. It's really just the way they look that bothers me. There's no physical feeling attached to it at all.\n\nDoctor: When you think about what other people have told you—that they don't see the wrinkles or the crooked face—do you believe them, or do you think they're just being nice and not telling you the truth about how you actually look?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I guess I know they mean well. When my coworkers say they don't see it, part of me thinks they're just being polite. You know, trying not to hurt my feelings. But then, my husband says the exact same thing, and he's usually pretty honest with me. \n\nSo, I don't know. Sometimes I think maybe I am blowing it out of proportion, but when I look in the mirror or see those pictures, it just looks so obvious to me. It's hard to believe they really don't see what I'm seeing.\n\nDoctor: When you're looking at yourself in the mirror or at those photos, deep down do you think the wrinkles and crookedness you're seeing are really as bad as they feel in that moment, or do you suspect you might be seeing them as worse than they actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, it's hard to say. I guess... I mean, when I'm right there looking at it, it feels completely real. It looks terrible to me. But when I step away from the mirror, and I think about what my husband said, or what the girls at work said... I do wonder if maybe my mind is playing tricks on me. Like maybe I'm zeroing in on it so much that it just seems bigger to me than it really is. I don't know for sure, but I do think there's a chance I might be making it worse in my head.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts and worries about your hands and face looking this way? It feels like it just sort of crept up on me over time.\n\nDoctor: If I told you that I looked at those photos and your hands and face look completely normal to me, would you be able to accept that, or would you still be convinced that there's something wrong with how you look?\n\nPatient: I think... I'm not sure if it's just a half hour or if it adds up to more than that throughout the day. I just haven't kept track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: When you really think about it honestly, do you believe that other people genuinely cannot see the wrinkles and crookedness you see, or do you think they're just not being truthful with you about what they actually observe?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know.     "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Body Dysmorphic Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror right now and see those wrinkles and the crookedness in your face, how convinced are you that it's actually there—are you 100% certain it's real, or is there some doubt?\n\nPatient: I... well, I guess I'm just not completely sure anymore. I mean, when I look in the mirror, I see it. It looks real to me. But... I don't know, maybe I'm not 100% certain. It's hard to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: When you really think about it, do you recognize that your worry about these wrinkles and the crookedness in your face might be excessive or out of proportion to what's actually there, or do you think your concerns are completely justified?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess I'm not really sure about that either. When I look at myself, it feels completely justified to me because of what I see. But... well, since I'm not even 100% sure what's actually there anymore, I suppose my worry could be a little out of proportion. I just don't know for a fact if it's excessive or not.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, how many hours would you say you're thinking about or worrying about how your hands and face look? Is it something that pops into your mind here and there, or are you thinking about it for hours throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Well, putting an exact number of hours on it is a little tough. But it's definitely more than just popping into my head here and there. Actually, that's really why I came in. \n\nSince I started helping out with the safety trainings at work, I just... I can’t seem to stop noticing it. When I’m up there pointing things out on the floor, all I can focus on is how wrinkly and uneven my hands look. It stays on my mind a lot while I'm trying to work. And then I start thinking about how my face looks all crooked in the company newsletter photos, even though the other workers tell me they don’t see it at all. \n\nSo, I guess if you add it up, it's quite a few hours throughout the day where I'm just distracted by it and worrying about how I look to everyone else.\n\n[Last Question]\nIf you were to see someone else with the same wrinkles and unevenness in their face and hands that you see in yourself, would you think that person looked as bad as you think you look, or would you think they looked perfectly normal?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / production_worker / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... I don't really know. I've never thought about it like that. I guess if I saw someone else with hands or a face like mine, I might just think they look like a normal person who's getting older. I don't know if I'd stare at them or think they look terrible. But when it's me... I just can't stand it. It feels different when it's my own face and hands.

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic? What's been concerning you?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Thank you. Well, I came in because... I guess lately I just can't seem to stop focusing on my appearance. \n\nI recently started helping out with the safety trainings at the plant where I work. But when I'm up there pointing things out to everyone, I just can't stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look. It really bothers me. And then... well, they put pictures of the trainings in the company newsletter, and when I look at them, I start thinking my face looks crooked in the photos. \n\nOther people tell me they don't see it at all, but I just can't seem to let it go. It's on my mind a lot, so I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How much is this concern about your hands and face affecting your daily life? For example, is it interfering with your work, your ability to be around others, or how much time you're spending thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped me from doing my job or helping out with the trainings. I'm still doing all of that. But I have noticed it takes me longer to get ready on the days I have to stand up in front of everyone. I spend a lot of time trying to make sure I look okay, maybe trying to cover up my hands a bit or fix my hair so my face doesn't look so uneven. \n\nAnd I've definitely started trying to avoid the camera when they're taking pictures for the newsletter. I'll just step out of the frame or turn away. I guess I've also been a little more hesitant about going to some of the get-togethers after work. I still go sometimes, but I just don't enjoy it as much as I used to because I'm always thinking about how I look. So it's not ruining my life, but it's definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands and face, do you notice any actual physical sensations like pain, tingling, numbness, or tension in those areas, or is it mainly just the way they look that bothers you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any pain or tingling or anything like that. My hands don't hurt, and my face feels completely normal. It's really just the way they look that bothers me. There's no physical feeling attached to it at all.\n\nDoctor: When you think about what other people have told you—that they don't see the wrinkles or the crooked face—do you believe them, or do you think they're just being nice and not telling you the truth about how you actually look?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I guess I know they mean well. When my coworkers say they don't see it, part of me thinks they're just being polite. You know, trying not to hurt my feelings. But then, my husband says the exact same thing, and he's usually pretty honest with me. \n\nSo, I don't know. Sometimes I think maybe I am blowing it out of proportion, but when I look in the mirror or see those pictures, it just looks so obvious to me. It's hard to believe they really don't see what I'm seeing.\n\nDoctor: When you're looking at yourself in the mirror or at those photos, deep down do you think the wrinkles and crookedness you're seeing are really as bad as they feel in that moment, or do you suspect you might be seeing them as worse than they actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, it's hard to say. I guess... I mean, when I'm right there looking at it, it feels completely real. It looks terrible to me. But when I step away from the mirror, and I think about what my husband said, or what the girls at work said... I do wonder if maybe my mind is playing tricks on me. Like maybe I'm zeroing in on it so much that it just seems bigger to me than it really is. I don't know for sure, but I do think there's a chance I might be making it worse in my head.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts and worries about your hands and face looking this way? When did this all start?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure when it started, to be honest. It's just been a while now. I can't really put a specific date or even a year on it. It feels like it just sort of crept up on me over time.\n\nDoctor: If I told you that I looked at those photos and your hands and face look completely normal to me, would you be able to accept that, or would you still be convinced that there's something wrong with how you look?\n\nPatient: I think... I think I'd try to believe you. If you, a doctor, are telling me that I look normal, I know I should trust that. But... I'd still have that nagging feeling. Even if I say I accept it, when I go home and look in the mirror again, I know I'll still see it. So, I guess I'd want to believe you, but it would be really hard to just completely let go of the idea that something is wrong.\n\nDoctor: When you're spending that extra time getting ready on training days or avoiding photos, would you say you're spending maybe 30 minutes to an hour on this, or is it taking up significantly more time than that—like several hours a day?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I haven't really looked at the clock to see exactly how long it takes. I'm not sure if it's just a half hour or if it adds up to more than that throughout the day. I just know I spend extra time trying to cover things up before I go out, especially on those training days, but... yeah, I really couldn't give you a specific number of minutes or hours. I just haven't kept track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: When you really think about it honestly, do you believe that other people genuinely cannot see the wrinkles and crookedness you see, or do you think they're just not being truthful with you about what they actually observe?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... I guess I haven't really figured that out. Sometimes I think maybe they genuinely just don't notice it, like they aren't looking at me that closely. But then... well, I don't know, maybe they do see it and they're just being polite by not bringing it up. I really couldn't tell you for sure what they actually see. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: If you were to rate on a scale of 0 to 100, where 0 means 'I'm completely certain my hands and face look exactly as bad as I perceive them' and 100 means 'I'm completely certain I'm exaggerating how bad they look,' where would you place yourself right now?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to put a number on it. I guess lately I just can't seem to stop focusing on my appearance. \n\nI recently started helping out with the safety trainings at the plant where I work. And then... well, they put pictures of the trainings in the company newsletter, and when I look at them, I start thinking my face looks crooked in the photos. \n\nOther people tell me they don't see it at all, but I just can't seem to let it go. It's on my mind a lot, so I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How much is this concern about your hands and face affecting your daily life? For example, is it interfering with your work, your ability to be around others, or how much time you're spending thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped me from doing my job or helping out with the trainings. I'm still doing all of that. But I have noticed it takes me longer to get ready on the days I have to stand up in front of everyone. I spend a lot of time trying to make sure I look okay, maybe trying to cover up my hands a bit or fix my hair so my face doesn't look so uneven. \n\nAnd I've definitely started trying to avoid the camera when they're taking pictures for the newsletter. I'll just step out of the frame or turn away. I guess I've also been a little more hesitant about going to some of the get-togethers after work. I still go sometimes, but I just don't enjoy it as much as I used to because I'm always thinking about how I look. So it's not ruining my life, but it's definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands and face, do you notice any actual physical sensations like pain, tingling, numbness, or tension in those areas, or is it mainly just the way they look that bothers you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any pain or tingling or anything like that. My hands don't hurt, and my face feels completely normal. It's really just the way they look that bothers me. There's no physical feeling attached to it at all.\n\nDoctor: When you think about what other people have told you—that they don't see the wrinkles or the crooked face—do you believe them, or do you think they're just being nice and not telling you the truth about how you actually look?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I guess I know they mean well. When my coworkers say they don't see it, part of me thinks they're just being polite. You know, trying not to hurt my feelings. But then, my husband says the exact same thing, and he's usually pretty honest with me. \n\nSo, I don't know. Sometimes I think maybe I am blowing it out of proportion, but when I look in the mirror or see those pictures, it just looks so obvious to me. It's hard to believe they really don't see what I'm seeing.\n\nDoctor: When you're looking at yourself in the mirror or at those photos, deep down do you think the wrinkles and crookedness you're seeing are really as bad as they feel in that moment, or do you suspect you might be seeing them as worse than they actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, it's hard to say. I guess... I mean, when I'm right there looking at it, it feels completely real. It looks terrible to me. But when I step away from the mirror, and I think about what my husband said, or what the girls at work said... I do wonder if maybe my mind is playing tricks on me. Like maybe I'm zeroing in on it so much that it just seems bigger to me than it really is. I don't know for sure, but I do think there's a chance I might be making it worse in my head.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts and worries about your hands and face looking this way? When did this all start?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure when it started, to be honest. It's just been a while now. I can't really put a specific date or even a year on it. It feels like it just sort of crept up on me over time.\n\nDoctor: If I told you that I looked at those photos and your hands and face look completely normal to me, would you be able to accept that, or would you still be convinced that there's something wrong with how you look?\n\nPatient: I think... ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... I'm really not sure. I mean, when I look in the mirror, I see it. It looks so real to me, the wrinkles and how my face isn't even. I don't know what to believe anymore. It's confusing.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.9, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic? What's been concerning you?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Thank you. Well, I came in because... I guess lately I just can't seem to stop focusing on my appearance. \n\nI recently started helping out with the safety trainings at the plant where I work. But when I'm up there pointing things out to everyone, I just can't stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look. It really bothers me. And then... well, they put pictures of the trainings in the company newsletter, and when I look at them, I start thinking my face looks crooked in the photos. \n\nOther people tell me they don't see it at all, but I just can't seem to let it go. It's on my mind a lot, so I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How much is this concern about your hands and face affecting your daily life? For example, is it interfering with your work, your ability to be around others, or how much time you're spending thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped me from doing my job or helping out with the trainings. I'm still doing all of that. But I have noticed it takes me longer to get ready on the days I have to stand up in front of everyone. I spend a lot of time trying to make sure I look okay, maybe trying to cover up my hands a bit or fix my hair so my face doesn't look so uneven. \n\nAnd I've definitely started trying to avoid the camera when they're taking pictures for the newsletter. I'll just step out of the frame or turn away. I guess I've also been a little more hesitant about going to some of the get-togethers after work. I still go sometimes, but I just don't enjoy it as much as I used to because I'm always thinking about how I look. So it's not ruining my life, but it's definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands and face, do you notice any actual physical sensations like pain, tingling, numbness, or tension in those areas, or is it mainly just the way they look that bothers you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any pain or tingling or anything like that. My hands don't hurt, and my face feels completely normal. It's really just the way they look that bothers me. There's no physical feeling attached to it at all.\n\nDoctor: When you think about what other people have told you—that they don't see the wrinkles or the crooked face—do you believe them, or do you think they're just being nice and not telling you the truth about how you actually look?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I guess I know they mean well. When my coworkers say they don't see it, part of me thinks they're just being polite. You know, trying not to hurt my feelings. But then, my husband says the exact same thing, and he's usually pretty honest with me. \n\nSo, I don't know. Sometimes I think maybe I am blowing it out of proportion, but when I look in the mirror or see those pictures, it just looks so obvious to me. It's hard to believe they really don't see what I'm seeing.\n\nDoctor: When you're looking at yourself in the mirror or at those photos, deep down do you think the wrinkles and crookedness you're seeing are really as bad as they feel in that moment, or do you suspect you might be seeing them as worse than they actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, it's hard to say. I guess... I mean, when I'm right there looking at it, it feels completely real. It looks terrible to me. But when I step away from the mirror, and I think about what my husband said, or what the girls at work said... I do wonder if maybe my mind is playing tricks on me. Like maybe I'm zeroing in on it so much that it just seems bigger to me than it really is. I don't know for sure, but I do think there's a chance I might be making it worse in my head.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts and worries about your hands and face looking this way? When did this all start?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure when it started, to be honest. It's just been a while now. I can't really put a specific date or even a year on it. It feels like it just sort of crept up on me over time.\n\nDoctor: If I told you that I looked at those photos and your hands and face look completely normal to me, would you be able to accept that, or would you still be convinced that there's something wrong with how you look?\n\nPatient: I think... I think I'd try to believe you. If you, a doctor, are telling me that I look normal, I know I should trust that. But... I'd still have that nagging feeling. Even if I say I accept it, when I go home and look in the mirror again, I know I'll still see it. So, I guess I'd want to believe you, but it would be really hard to just completely let go of the idea that something is wrong.\n\nDoctor: When you're spending that extra time getting ready on training days or avoiding photos, would you say you're spending maybe 30 minutes to an hour on this, or is it taking up significantly more time than that—like several hours a day?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I haven't really looked at the clock to see exactly how long it takes. I'm not sure if it's just a half hour or if it adds up to more than that throughout the day. I just know I spend extra time trying to cover things up before I go out, especially on those training days, but... yeah, I really couldn't give you a specific number of minutes or hours. I just haven't kept track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: When you really think about it honestly, do you believe that other people genuinely cannot see the wrinkles and crookedness you see, or do you think they're just not being truthful with you about what they actually observe?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... I guess I haven't really figured that out. Sometimes I think maybe they genuinely just don't notice it, like they aren't looking at me that closely. But then... well, I don't know, maybe they do see it and they're just being polite by not bringing it up. I really couldn't tell you for sure what they actually see. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: If you were to rate on a scale of 0 to 100, where 0 means 'I'm completely certain my hands and face look exactly as bad as I perceive them' and 100 means 'I'm completely certain I'm exaggerating how bad they look,' where would you place yourself right now?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to put a number on it. I guess I'm just not sure. I mean, I know what I see when I look in the mirror, but after what you said... I don't know if I'm completely certain about anything right now. I just couldn't give you a number on a scale like that.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror right now and see those wrinkles and the crookedness in your face, how convinced are you that it's actually there—are you 100% certain it's real, or is there some doubt?\n\nPatient: I... well, I guess I'm just not completely sure anymore. I mean, when I look in the mirror, I see it. It looks real to me. But... I don't know, maybe I'm not 100% certain. It's hard to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: When you really think about it, do you recognize that your worry about these wrinkles and the crookedness in your face might be excessive or out of proportion to what's actually there, or do you think your concerns are completely justified?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess I'm not really sure about that either. When I look at myself, it feels completely justified to me because of what I see. But... well, since I'm not even 100% sure what's actually there anymore, I suppose my worry could be a little out of proportion. I just don't know for a fact if it's excessive or not.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, how many hours would you say you're thinking about or worrying about how your hands and face look? Is it something that pops into your mind here and there, or are you thinking about it for hours throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Well, putting an exact number of hours on it is a little tough. But it's definitely more than just popping into my head here and there. Actually, that's really why I came in. \n\nSince I started helping out with the safety trainings at work, I just... I can’t seem to stop noticing it. When I’m up there pointing things out on the floor, all I can focus on is how wrinkly and uneven my hands look. It stays on my mind a lot while I'm trying to work. And then I start thinking about how my face looks all crooked in the company newsletter photos, even though the other workers tell me they don’t see it at all. \n\nSo, I guess if you add it up, it's quite a few hours throughout the day where I'm just distracted by it and worrying about how I look to everyone else.\n\nDoctor: If you were to see someone else with the same wrinkles and unevenness in their face and hands that you see in yourself, would you think that person looked as bad as you think you look, or would you think they looked perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I've never thought about it like that. I guess if I saw someone else with hands or a face like mine, I might just think they look like a normal person who's getting older. I don't know if I'd stare at them or think they look terrible. But when it's me... I just can't stand it. It feels different when it's my own face and hands.\n\nDoctor: When your husband and coworkers tell you they don't see the wrinkles or crookedness, do you truly believe that they actually cannot see these things, or do you think they can see them but are just choosing not to tell you?\n\nPatient: I really don't know for sure. Sometimes I think they must see it and they're just being polite, you know, trying not to hurt my feelings. But then other times... they seem so genuine when they say they don't notice anything. So I guess I'm not entirely sure what they actually see.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror and see those wrinkles and crookedness, is it possible that these things might not actually be there at all—that your mind might be creating or exaggerating something that doesn't really exist—or are you quite certain that they definitely do exist, even if others claim they don't see them?\n\nPatient: I... I'm really not sure. I mean, when I look in the mirror, I see it. It looks so real to me, the wrinkles and how my face isn't even. But when everyone else tells me they don't see it, it does make me wonder. I guess it's possible my mind is playing tricks on me or making it seem worse than it is. I just... I don't know what to believe anymore. It's confusing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I can’t seem to stop noticing it. When I’m up there pointing things out on the floor, all I can focus on is how wrinkly and uneven my hands look. It stays on my mind a lot while I'm trying to work. And then I start thinking about how my face looks all crooked in the company newsletter photos, even though the other workers tell me they don’t see it at all. \n\nSo, I guess if you add it up, it's quite a few hours throughout the day where I'm just distracted by it and worrying about how I look to everyone else.\n\nDoctor: If you were to see someone else with the same wrinkles and unevenness in their face and hands that you see in yourself, would you think that person looked as bad as you think you look, or would you think they looked perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I've never thought about it like that. I guess if I saw someone else with hands or a face like mine, I might just think they look like a normal person who's getting older. I don't know if I'd stare at them or think they look terrible. But when it's me... I just can't stand it. It feels different when it's my own face and hands.\n\nDoctor: When your husband and coworkers tell you they don't see the wrinkles or crookedness, do you truly believe that they actually cannot see these things, or do you think they can see them but are just choosing not to tell you?\n\nPatient: I really don't know for sure. Sometimes I think they must see it and they're just being polite, you know, trying not to hurt my feelings. But then other times... they seem so genuine when they say they don't notice anything. So I guess I'm not entirely sure what they actually see.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror and see those wrinkles and crookedness, is it possible that these things might not actually be there at all—that your mind might be creating or exaggerating something that doesn't really exist—or are you quite certain that they definitely do exist, even if others claim they don't see them?\n\nPatient: I... I'm really not sure. I mean, when I look in the mirror, I see it. It looks so real to me, the wrinkles and how my face isn't even. But when everyone else tells me they don't see it, it does make me wonder. I guess it's possible my mind is playing tricks on me or making it seem worse than it is. I just... I don't know what to believe anymore. It's confusing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic? What's been concerning you?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Thank you. Well, I came in because... I guess lately I just can't seem to stop focusing on my appearance. \n\nI recently started helping out with the safety trainings at the plant where I work. But when I'm up there pointing things out to everyone, I just can't stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look. It really bothers me. And then... well, they put pictures of the trainings in the company newsletter, and when I look at them, I start thinking my face looks crooked in the photos. \n\nOther people tell me they don't see it at all, but I just can't seem to let it go. It's on my mind a lot, so I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How much is this concern about your hands and face affecting your daily life? For example, is it interfering with your work, your ability to be around others, or how much time you're spending thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped me from doing my job or helping out with the trainings. I'm still doing all of that. But I have noticed it takes me longer to get ready on the days I have to stand up in front of everyone. I spend a lot of time trying to make sure I look okay, maybe trying to cover up my hands a bit or fix my hair so my face doesn't look so uneven. \n\nAnd I've definitely started trying to avoid the camera when they're taking pictures for the newsletter. I'll just step out of the frame or turn away. I guess I've also been a little more hesitant about going to some of the get-togethers after work. I still go sometimes, but I just don't enjoy it as much as I used to because I'm always thinking about how I look. So it's not ruining my life, but it's definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your hands and face, do you notice any actual physical sensations like pain, tingling, numbness, or tension in those areas, or is it mainly just the way they look that bothers you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any pain or tingling or anything like that. My hands don't hurt, and my face feels completely normal. It's really just the way they look that bothers me. There's no physical feeling attached to it at all.\n\nDoctor: When you think about what other people have told you—that they don't see the wrinkles or the crooked face—do you believe them, or do you think they're just being nice and not telling you the truth about how you actually look?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I guess I know they mean well. When my coworkers say they don't see it, part of me thinks they're just being polite. You know, trying not to hurt my feelings. But then, my husband says the exact same thing, and he's usually pretty honest with me. \n\nSo, I don't know. Sometimes I think maybe I am blowing it out of proportion, but when I look in the mirror or see those pictures, it just looks so obvious to me. It's hard to believe they really don't see what I'm seeing.\n\nDoctor: When you're looking at yourself in the mirror or at those photos, deep down do you think the wrinkles and crookedness you're seeing are really as bad as they feel in that moment, or do you suspect you might be seeing them as worse than they actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, it's hard to say. I guess... I mean, when I'm right there looking at it, it feels completely real. It looks terrible to me. But when I step away from the mirror, and I think about what my husband said, or what the girls at work said... I do wonder if maybe my mind is playing tricks on me. Like maybe I'm zeroing in on it so much that it just seems bigger to me than it really is. I don't know for sure, but I do think there's a chance I might be making it worse in my head.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts and worries about your hands and face looking this way? When did this all start?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure when it started, to be honest. It's just been a while now. I can't really put a specific date or even a year on it. It feels like it just sort of crept up on me over time.\n\nDoctor: If I told you that I looked at those photos and your hands and face look completely normal to me, would you be able to accept that, or would you still be convinced that there's something wrong with how you look?\n\nPatient: I think... I think I'd try to believe you. If you, a doctor, are telling me that I look normal, I know I should trust that. But... I'd still have that nagging feeling. Even if I say I accept it, when I go home and look in the mirror again, I know I'll still see it. So, I guess I'd want to believe you, but it would be really hard to just completely let go of the idea that something is wrong.\n\nDoctor: When you're spending that extra time getting ready on training days or avoiding photos, would you say you're spending maybe 30 minutes to an hour on this, or is it taking up significantly more time than that—like several hours a day?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I haven't really looked at the clock to see exactly how long it takes. I'm not sure if it's just a half hour or if it adds up to more than that throughout the day. I just know I spend extra time trying to cover things up before I go out, especially on those training days, but... yeah, I really couldn't give you a specific number of minutes or hours. I just haven't kept track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: When you really think about it honestly, do you believe that other people genuinely cannot see the wrinkles and crookedness you see, or do you think they're just not being truthful with you about what they actually observe?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... I guess I haven't really figured that out. Sometimes I think maybe they genuinely just don't notice it, like they aren't looking at me that closely. But then... well, I don't know, maybe they do see it and they're just being polite by not bringing it up. I really couldn't tell you for sure what they actually see. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: If you were to rate on a scale of 0 to 100, where 0 means 'I'm completely certain my hands and face look exactly as bad as I perceive them' and 100 means 'I'm completely certain I'm exaggerating how bad they look,' where would you place yourself right now?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to put a number on it. I guess I'm just not sure. I mean, I know what I see when I look in the mirror, but after what you said... I don't know if I'm completely certain about anything right now. I just couldn't give you a number on a scale like that.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror right now and see those wrinkles and the crookedness in your face, how convinced are you that it's actually there—are you 100% certain it's real, or is there some doubt?\n\nPatient: I... well, I guess I'm just not completely sure anymore. I mean, when I look in the mirror, I see it. It looks real to me. But... I don't know, maybe I'm not 100% certain. It's hard to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: When you really think about it, do you recognize that your worry about these wrinkles and the crookedness in your face might be excessive or out of proportion to what's actually there, or do you think your concerns are completely justified?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess I'm not really sure about that either. When I look at myself, it feels completely justified to me because of what I see. But... well, since I'm not even 100% sure what's actually there anymore, I suppose my worry could be a little out of proportion. I just don't know for a fact if it's excessive or not.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, how many hours would you say you're thinking about or worrying about how your hands and face look? Is it something that pops into your mind here and there, or are you thinking about it for hours throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Well, putting an exact number of hours on it is a little tough. I think I'd try to believe you. If you, a doctor, are telling me that I look normal, I know I should trust that. But... I'd still have that nagging feeling. Even if I say I accept it, when I go home and look in the mirror again, I know I'll still see it. So, I guess I'd want to believe you, but it would be really hard to just completely let go of the idea that something is wrong.\n\nDoctor: When you're spending that extra time getting ready on training days or avoiding photos, would you say you're spending maybe 30 minutes to an hour on this, or is it taking up significantly more time than that—like several hours a day?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I haven't really looked at the clock to see exactly how long it takes. I'm not sure if it's just a half hour or if it adds up to more than that throughout the day. I just know I spend extra time trying to cover things up before I go out, especially on those training days, but... yeah, I really couldn't give you a specific number of minutes or hours. I just haven't kept track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: When you really think about it honestly, do you believe that other people genuinely cannot see the wrinkles and crookedness you see, or do you think they're just not being truthful with you about what they actually observe?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... I guess I haven't really figured that out. Sometimes I think maybe they genuinely just don't notice it, like they aren't looking at me that closely. But then... well, I don't know, maybe they do see it and they're just being polite by not bringing it up. I really couldn't tell you for sure what they actually see. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: If you were to rate on a scale of 0 to 100, where 0 means 'I'm completely certain my hands and face look exactly as bad as I perceive them' and 100 means 'I'm completely certain I'm exaggerating how bad they look,' where would you place yourself right now?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to put a number on it. I guess I'm just not sure. I mean, I know what I see when I look in the mirror, but after what you said... I don't know if I'm completely certain about anything right now. I just couldn't give you a number on a scale like that.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror right now and see those wrinkles and the crookedness in your face, how convinced are you that it's actually there—are you 100% certain it's real, or is there some doubt?\n\nPatient: I... well, I guess I'm just not completely sure anymore. I mean, when I look in the mirror, I see it. It looks real to me. But... I don't know, maybe I'm not 100% certain. It's hard to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: When you really think about it, do you recognize that your worry about these wrinkles and the crookedness in your face might be excessive or out of proportion to what's actually there, or do you think your concerns are completely justified?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess I'm not really sure about that either. When I look at myself, it feels completely justified to me because of what I see. But... well, since I'm not even 100% sure what's actually there anymore, I suppose my worry could be a little out of proportion. I just don't know for a fact if it's excessive or not.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, how many hours would you say you're thinking about or worrying about how your hands and face look? Is it something that pops into your mind here and there, or are you thinking about it for hours throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Well, putting an exact number of hours on it is a little tough. But it's definitely more than just popping into my head here and there. Actually, that's really why I came in. \n\nSince I started helping out with the safety trainings at work, I just... I can’t seem to stop noticing it. When I’m up there pointing things out on the floor, all I can focus on is how wrinkly and uneven my hands look. It stays on my mind a lot while I'm trying to work. And then I start thinking about how my face looks all crooked in the company newsletter photos, even though the other workers tell me they don’t see it at all. \n\nSo, I guess if you add it up, it's quite a few hours throughout the day where I'm just distracted by it and worrying about how I look to everyone else.\n\nDoctor: If you were to see someone else with the same wrinkles and unevenness in their face and hands that you see in yourself, would you think that person looked as bad as you think you look, or would you think they looked perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I've never thought about it like that. I guess if I saw someone else with hands or a face like mine, I might just think they look like a normal person who's getting older. I don't know if I'd stare at them or think they look terrible. But when it's me... I just can't stand it. It feels different when it's my own face and hands.\n\nDoctor: When your husband and coworkers tell you they don't see the wrinkles or crookedness, do you truly believe that they actually cannot see these things, or do you think they can see them but are just choosing not to tell you?\n\nPatient: I really don't know for sure. Sometimes I think they must see it and they're just being polite, you know, trying not to hurt my feelings. But then other times... they seem so genuine when they say they don't notice anything. So I guess I'm not entirely sure what they actually see.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror and see those wrinkles and crookedness, is it possible that these things might not actually be there at all—that your mind might be creating or exaggerating something that doesn't really exist—or are you quite certain that they definitely do exist, even if others claim they don't see them?\n\nPatient: I... I'm really not sure. I mean, when I look in the mirror, I see it. It looks so real to me, the wrinkles and how my face isn't even. But when everyone else tells me they don't see it, it does make me wonder. I guess it's possible my mind is playing tricks on me or making it seem worse than it is. I just... I don't know what to believe anymore. It's confusing.\n\nDoctor: When your husband and coworkers say they don't see the wrinkles or crookedness, do you think they are telling you the truth about what they actually observe, or do you believe they are lying to spare your feelings?\n\nPatient: I think I just answered that, honestly. Like I said, I'm really not sure. Sometimes I think they're just being polite and lying to me so I don't feel bad. But other times, they seem like they're telling the truth and they really just don't see it. I go back and forth on it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   "is_final": true
